## Implementation plan
# Design: Situation Room, a firedrill board for an enterprise IT lead

Generated by /office-hours on 2026-11-10
Branch: main
Repo: pcarpe4/situation-room
Status: APPROVED
Mode: Startup (intrapreneurship: internal tool, the sponsor is the author's boss)

Nothing in this document was tested against your tenant or cluster. Licensing and platform facts come from public docs read on 2026-11-10 and are listed under References.

## Problem Statement

An enterprise IT lead (the author's boss) has to keep tabs on a steady stream of "firedrills": urgent cross-team asks such as ServiceNow tickets, a mandate that every application implement functional tests, onboarding those tests to another platform, and service account password rotations. They arrive by email from many senders with no shared pattern, so a mail rule cannot catch them. He spots them by reading his inbox, then gets status by pinging his managers one at a time. There is no shared record of what is open, who owns it, or when it last moved.

Situation Room is one place where he sees every open firedrill with a short description, owner, due date and latest status, and where his managers post updates.

## Demand Evidence

- The boss asked for this (the author's statement in session). No direct quote was captured, so the working reading is "asked for one place to see status", not "asked for a custom app".
- No manager has been asked about posting updates. Their participation rests on the boss requiring it.
- Still missing: his own words, one named firedrill that was dropped or went stale, and one manager's reaction. The Assignment collects these.

## Status Quo

- Intake: he reads email and decides what is a firedrill. This works and stays (Premise 2).
- Tracking: his inbox and memory. No spreadsheet, SharePoint list, Planner board or ServiceNow view has been tried.
- Updates: he emails or messages each manager. Answers live in threads.
- Cost: not measured. How many firedrills are open at once, how often he asks, and how many get dropped are unknown.

## Target User & Narrowest Wedge

- Reader: the boss. He wins if he can answer "what is open, who has it, is it moving" in under a minute without sending an email.
- Writers: his managers. They answer when asked. They will be told to use it. What they get back is fewer pings, a visible answer when they need something from him ("Needs you"), and a mark showing their update was read.
- Triage: the author. Confirms drafts and keeps it running.
- Narrowest wedge inside the chosen approach: Milestone 1. A board, paste-in intake with rule-based prefill, a human confirm step, a one-screen manager update, and a ping that opens a prefilled email. It needs a namespace, a database, login, and somewhere to build and store the image. It does not need mailbox access, a model endpoint or mail-sending rights. The CEO review added six small items to Milestone 1 (see Milestones), so it is no longer the smallest possible first version. That was the author's choice.

## Constraints

- Runs on OpenShift and keeps state across restarts.
- Active Directory login and role-based access.
- Power Automate: built-in and standard connectors only. No premium connectors, custom connectors or AI Builder.
- Firedrills come from many senders. There is no distribution list or subject convention to filter on.
- One maintainer (the author).
- Not yet known: whether the cluster is on-prem or cloud, whether "AD" means Entra ID or on-prem AD, whether pods can reach graph.microsoft.com, whether an approved model endpoint exists, and which CI system and image registry the org uses.

## Premises

All six were confirmed in session.

1. **Success test.** Version one works if the boss stops asking managers for status by email. Not if it looks good, and not if it parses email well.
2. **A person picks the firedrills.** The boss (or the author) forwards or pastes the email to start one. The system never reads his inbox on its own.
3. **Suggest, then confirm.** Field extraction produces a draft. A person approves or fixes it before it shows on the board. This premise governs intake. A manager's reply to an ask (M2) posts as an update with no confirm step, because the manager is its author and gets a receipt quoting exactly what was posted.
4. **Pull, not push.** On a standard license a flow cannot POST to an outside API (the HTTP action and custom connectors are premium), and an internal OpenShift route is likely unreachable from Microsoft's cloud anyway. So email lands in Microsoft 365 and the app pulls it.
5. **Ping-driven updates.** Managers update when asked, not on their own. The board sends the ask, and answering is one step. From M2 the board can send the ask without the boss clicking. "Needs you" and "Seen by" give managers a reason to post unasked, but the plan does not count on that.
6. **The cheap version is a real contender.** A Microsoft List plus two standard flows (Approach A) has never been tried here. The custom app has to beat it on two things: drafting fields from the email, and the ask-and-answer loop with visible staleness.

## Approaches Considered

- **A) Microsoft List + two standard flows.** One list with a row per firedrill (title, description, owner, due date, status, last update). One flow creates a row when the boss moves an email into a set Outlook folder, and the other reminds each owner on a schedule to update their rows. Not chosen by the author. It has no field extraction and is not a codebase the author owns. It is the fallback: if Milestone 1's prerequisites are not granted within three weeks of filing, ship A for the boss and keep the requests open.
- **B) Custom app on OpenShift with pull intake.** Chosen by the author.
- **C) List now, app next.** Recommended in session, not chosen. The author went straight to the app. The cost of that choice (the boss waits, adoption is untested until launch) is reduced by keeping Milestone 1's prerequisites short and by the three-week fallback above.

## Recommended Approach

Approach B, built in milestones so the boss has a working board before the slow approvals (mailbox access, model endpoint) land.

Wireframe: ~/.gstack/projects/pcarpe4-situation-room/patrick-main-sketch-20261110-192633.png (drawn before the reviews. It is out of date and is redrawn before the Assignment, see "Design amendments")

### Shape

One deployable: a server-rendered web app backed by Postgres. A JSON API is not part of Milestone 1 and is added when it has its first caller (see "API"). Proposed stack: Python, FastAPI, server-rendered templates with HTMX, SQLAlchemy and Alembic. Reasons: Python's standard `email` package parses .eml files and MIME threads, one process serves the pages and, later, the JSON form, and there is no separate front-end build. If your team already runs another stack on OpenShift, use that. Nothing below depends on Python.

### Screens (from the wireframe)

The design review of 2026-11-11 specifies these screens in more detail, adds a fourth (Firedrill) and a landing view for managers, and sets the states each one shows. That detail is in the "Design amendments" list at the end of this plan. Where it differs from the three descriptions below, the list governs.

1. **Board.** Counts across the top double as filters: open, stale, asked with no reply, waiting, drafts to confirm, due this week, overdue. Open, due this week and overdue count firedrills. Stale, asked with no reply and waiting count assignments, and each of those filters shows the firedrills that have at least one such assignment. Drafts to confirm counts intake items. A Closed filter, with no count, lists closed firedrills newest first with a Reopen action for a Lead or Triage. Above the rows, a "Needs you" list shows every open need a manager has raised, with its age and a Handled button. Managers open on "My assignments" (see "Design amendments") and can switch to the same board, including that list, without the Lead actions. Rows sort stale first. Rows that are not stale sort by status (blocked, at risk, new, on track), then soonest due, with undated firedrills after dated ones. Overdue rows are not pulled above stale ones: a stale row has no current information, which is the worse problem, and the overdue count is one click away. Each row shows title, one-line description, source, owner, due date, status and the last update's text and age, so he reads status without clicking. A past-due firedrill shows an overdue badge with the number of days. An assignment that is waiting shows "Waiting until" with its date and reason. Ticket refs are links to the ticket. A firedrill with several owners shows one line per owner inside its row, each with its own status, last update and ask button (the wireframe shows the single-owner case and predates the CEO review additions). The ask cell on each owner line shows one of the states listed for it under "Design amendments". Intake failures and failed mail sends show as a banner to Leads and Triage (a Lead's banner is narrowed under "Design amendments"). The banner holds only entries that still need an action, and Triage can dismiss any entry from the Health page. Empty states carry the copy listed under "Design amendments".
2. **Confirm a draft.** The email on the left with older replies folded. Drafted fields on the right, each showing where it came from. Fields the system could not find are empty and marked. At least one owner must be picked before publishing. When an open firedrill shares a ticket ref or the same subject, the screen shows it as a possible match. Actions: publish, attach to the matched firedrill, save draft, not a firedrill.
3. **Manager update.** Opened from one of the ask's links after sign-in, or from the Update button on the manager's own row. The ask carries four links, one per status, and the screen opens with that status already chosen. Shows the firedrill and the manager's last update, with "Seen by" and a time once a Lead has viewed it, and whether an earlier need was handled. Two inputs: a status choice (on track, at risk, blocked, done) and "what changed". One optional input: "need anything from your boss". The manager can also set "waiting until" a date with a reason, and withdraw a need they raised. An assignment that has ended accepts no update by any path: its link tells the former owner who has it now.

### Data model

- `person`: display_name, email, login_id, role (lead, manager, triage), active. `email` is the real mailbox address, used for the mailto recipient and the accepted-sender check. `login_id` is the value sign-in matches: `X-Forwarded-User` under oauth-proxy, or the provider's username claim under OIDC. The two can differ, so they are stored separately (Open Question 14). Every other table points at the person row, not at either value, so changing login method means updating `login_id` and orphans nothing. `email` and `login_id` are stored lowercased, are each unique, and are compared without regard to case. The first Triage person is created at deploy time: a deploy setting names their sign-in value and mailbox address, and the migration Job creates that one row only when no active Triage person exists, and corrects that seeded row's sign-in value when the deploy setting changes. In M1 that person enters the boss and the managers by hand in the app. A signed-in user with no active row gets no access. The no-access page shows the value that was presented, and each refused sign-in is recorded with it, so Triage can correct a roster entry from the Health page. A person cannot be made inactive while they own an active assignment (the firedrill is open and the assignment is neither done nor ended) or while they are the primary Lead: reassign first. Their done and closed history stays with them.
- `firedrill`: title, short_description, state (open, closed), due_date, requested_by, source, ticket_refs, auto_ask (on by default, a Lead can switch it off), auto_ask_changed_at, created_by, created_at, closed_at
- `assignment`: firedrill, owner (a person), status (new, on_track, at_risk, blocked, done), waiting_until, waiting_reason, created_at, ended_at (set when the owner is removed or replaced). One per owner. A firedrill has one or more that have not ended.
- `update`: assignment, author, on_behalf_of (empty unless Lead or Triage posted it for the owner), status (empty when an email reply names none), text, source (form, email reply), source_message_id (email replies only, so one reply is never posted twice), need_from_lead, need_handled_at, need_handled_by, need_withdrawn_at, seen_at, seen_by (the Lead who first viewed it), created_at
- `update_request`: assignment, asked_by (the person who clicked, or the primary Lead for an automatic ask), requested_at, answered_by_update, cancelled_at, origin (manual, auto), clock_start (automatic asks only: the date the stale clock was counting from, which names the stale period), reply_ref (M2, the reference a reply is matched on), mail_send (M2, app-sent asks only, the latest send). `firedrill.requested_by` is the email's requester, a different thing, which is why this field is not called `requested_by`.
- `mail_send` (M2): kind (ask, digest, receipt, notice, alert), recipient, about (the ask, update, inbound message or digest date it concerns, or the job or send an alert is about), reason (notices only), state (pending, sent, failed, cancelled, expired), attempts, created_at, last_attempt_at, expires_at, sent_at. Every email the app sends has a row, written before the send. A digest is unique per Monday date. The text of an email is built when it is sent, from the row and current data, so a late send is never out of date.
- `setting`: key, value, changed_by, changed_at. Holds the stale threshold N, the retention periods, the ServiceNow address, the primary Lead (the person asks are sent for and the digest goes to), the maintainer's alert address, the global automatic-asks switch and, from M2, the reply-to-update switch and the Monday digest switch. Triage changes settings in the app. The time zone is not a setting: it is deploy-time configuration read by the app and by every scheduled job, so day boundaries and the 7:00 run cannot disagree.
- `intake_item`: source (paste, eml, mailbox), raw content, content hash, external message id, received_at, state (new, drafted, published, attached, rejected, failed), extraction result, draft_fields, firedrill (set on publish or attach, so one firedrill can hold several emails), shared_with_owners (per email, off by default). A draft is the intake item itself. `extraction result` keeps what the rules, and later the model, produced. `draft_fields` holds the person's working values and picked owners from "save draft" until publish. `attached` means the email was stored on an existing firedrill. `failed` means normalizing or extraction raised an error: the item shows in the intake banner and can be retried or rejected.
- `audit_log`: who, what, when, for every write
- `usage_event`: person, day, kind (sign_in, board_view). One row per person per day per kind. This is the data source for the first success criterion.
- `job_run`: job (purge, five-minute, scheduler), started_at, finished_at, outcome (ok, failed), detail. One row per run. The Health page and the alerts read it. When the five-minute job makes up a missed 7:00 run, that work is recorded as a `scheduler` run.
- `refused_sign_in`: presented value, at. Kept 30 days.
- `inbound_message` (M2): the message's stable id, sender, received_at, kind (intake, reply, automatic, other), outcome (drafted, posted, not accepted, ignored), reason, the intake item or update it produced, raw content (replies only, deleted after 30 days). Every message the poller handles gets one row, written before the message is moved, so no message is handled twice even if the move fails.
- `weekly_figure`: week, name, value. The Friday figures under Success Criteria, stored each Friday by the daily job, so the stop rule reads a number that cannot drift afterwards.

Rules that follow from the model:

- **Ownership.** Status, updates and asks belong to an assignment, not to the firedrill. A mandate that touches four managers is one firedrill with four assignments. Publishing requires at least one assignment.
- **Status.** An assignment starts as `new` and takes the status of its latest update. An update with no status leaves the assignment's status as it was. The board shows a multi-owner firedrill at the worst status among its assignments that have not ended, in this order: blocked, at risk, new, on track. Done assignments are left out of that roll-up unless every assignment that has not ended is done.
- **Open and done.** A firedrill is open until Lead or Triage closes it. A manager choosing "done" marks only that assignment done. When every assignment that has not ended is done, the board shows the firedrill as "Done, ready to close" and it stays open until closed. Done assignments are never stale and cannot be asked. A done assignment becomes active again only when its owner, a Lead or Triage posts an update on the form with another status. An email reply never reopens one (see Reply-to-update). Closing a firedrill cancels its open asks and closes its open needs. A Lead or Triage can reopen a closed firedrill from the board's Closed filter, which lists closed firedrills newest first. Reopening clears `closed_at`, brings the assignments back with the status they had, and starts each one's stale clock at the reopen date. Asks and needs that the close ended stay ended. An assignment whose owner is no longer active is ended at reopen, and if that would leave the firedrill with no assignment, Reopen requires picking an active owner first. Owners can be changed only while a firedrill is open. Email content already deleted by the retention rules is not restored.
- **Active.** An assignment is active when its firedrill is open and it is neither done nor ended. Stale, fresh and asks apply to active assignments only. The freshness figure and the stop rule judge "active" when the Friday figure is computed, and the ask figures judge it when the ask was made.
- **Stale and fresh.** An active assignment that is not waiting is stale from the start of working day N+1 after the calendar date of its newest update (or of its own creation, if it has no update). Dates are taken in the app's time zone. Working days are Monday to Friday. N defaults to 3 and is a setting, so an update on a Monday makes the assignment stale on Friday morning. Fresh means active, not waiting and not stale. A firedrill is stale if any of its active assignments is.
- **Waiting.** The owner, a Lead or Triage can set `waiting_until` with a reason, and the reason is required. Setting or changing a wait is recorded as an update whose text is the reason, and clearing one early is recorded as an update that says so. Either answers a pending ask. When a Lead or Triage does it for the owner, the update carries `on_behalf_of`, like any other update posted for an owner. The assignment is waiting through the `waiting_until` date itself: it is not stale and is not asked automatically. From the next day the stale clock counts from that date or from the newest update, whichever is later. A later update does not end a wait. The owner, a Lead or Triage can clear it early, and a Lead can still ask by hand while it runs.
- **Overdue.** An open firedrill is overdue from the day after its due date, unless it is "Done, ready to close". The badge shows the calendar days past due. Overdue has its own count. "Due this week" counts due dates from today through Sunday, and a week runs Monday to Sunday in the app's time zone.
- **Needs.** A non-empty "need anything from your boss" is an open need until a Lead or Triage marks it handled. It also closes when its assignment ends or its firedrill closes, and the manager who raised it can withdraw it. Everyone signed in sees the "Needs you" list, and the manager who raised a need sees who handled it and when. A need can only be raised on the form: an email reply cannot raise one, and its receipt says so.
- **Seen.** An update is marked seen the first time a Lead opens a page that renders it. That includes the board, since each row shows its latest update. The mark records which Lead and when. The update's author sees it, and so does the owner when the update was posted for them. Seen marks and usage events are not written to the audit log.
- **Changing owners.** Lead or Triage can add or remove an owner after publish. Reassigning ends the old owner's assignment and creates a new one for the new owner. The ended assignment keeps its updates as history and drops off the board. A pending ask on it is cancelled, never carried over. The new assignment starts as `new`, and its staleness clock starts at its own creation. The app does not notify a new owner. The publish, add-owner and reassign steps each end on "Tell the owner": a prefilled email, on by default, that names the firedrill and links to it. From M2, the first app-sent ask on an assignment also says who assigned it and when.

### Intake (pull, not push)

| Path | Milestone | Needs |
|---|---|---|
| Paste into "New firedrill": a subject field plus a body field for the pasted chain | M1 | Nothing |
| Upload a saved .eml file (only if the boss's Outlook can save .eml, see Open Question 12) | M2 | Nothing |
| Mailbox pull: the boss forwards to one dedicated shared mailbox, the app polls Microsoft Graph for new messages there | M2 | Entra app registration, Exchange "RBAC for Applications" scoped to that one mailbox, egress from the cluster to Graph |

Every path creates an intake item, then a draft. In M1 the expected path is the one the author first described: the boss forwards the email to the author as he does today, and the author pastes and confirms it. The boss is not expected to use the paste form himself.

**Accepted senders.** The poller drafts a mailbox message only when its sender is an active Lead or Triage person. Anything else is moved to a "Not accepted" folder in the mailbox and listed on the Health page with its reason. The poller reads the Inbox only, and every message it handles leaves the Inbox: to "Processed" when it became a draft or an update, to "Not accepted" when it was refused for any reason (the sender, a missing reference, not the owner, an ended or done assignment, a closed firedrill, no usable text), and to "Automatic" for automatic mail and for replies to the app's own digests, receipts, notices and alerts. Each message gets an `inbound_message` row with its outcome and reason before it is moved. Ask the Exchange admin to also restrict delivery to the mailbox to those senders and, once reply-to-update ships, to the managers in the roster.

**Replies (M2).** A message that carries an ask's reference is never drafted as a firedrill, whoever sent it. From the owner of that assignment it becomes an update (see Updates and asks). From anyone else nothing is posted: an active roster member gets a short notice saying why, and any other sender gets nothing. A message from an active manager that carries no reference is not accepted either, and that manager gets a notice pointing to the board, where each of their assignments has an Update button, so nobody believes they answered when they did not. Digests, receipts, notices and alerts carry no ask reference. They set Reply-To to the app's mailbox and carry a fixed marker in the subject, and a message that carries that marker is never drafted, never posted and never answered: it is filed as `other`, ignored and moved to "Automatic", whoever sent it. Each of those mails says that replies to it are not read and gives the link to use. While the reply-to-update switch is off no reply is posted: a message that carries an ask's reference is filed as not accepted, and its sender, if an active roster member, gets a notice pointing to that assignment's update link.

**Automatic mail (M2).** A message marked as automatic (an out-of-office reply, a bounce, a read receipt) is never posted, never answers an ask and never triggers a receipt or a notice. A bounce that can be matched to something the app sent marks that send as failed, so a bounced ask shows "Ask failed" and a bounced receipt raises "receipt not delivered". An out-of-office reply to an open ask shows "automatic reply received" on that row. Everything the app sends is marked as auto-generated so other systems do not answer it. The app sends at most five notices to one address per day. Receipts for posted updates are never capped, because the receipt is what stands in for a confirm step.

**Duplicates.** The same mailbox message is never processed twice: its `inbound_message` row is keyed on the message's stable id (the Internet Message-ID header, which should not change when a message moves folders, to be proven on the tenant). Identical pasted text is flagged by a hash of the whitespace-normalized body. A chain forwarded again with a new reply is not an exact duplicate, so it becomes a draft, and the confirm screen offers "attach to the matched firedrill" when ticket refs or the subject match an open one. Attaching stores the email on that firedrill and creates nothing new.

Power Automate is optional here. A standard flow can forward a flagged email to the mailbox. It never calls the API.

### Extraction: turning a long email chain into fields

Each step is useful without the next one.

1. **Normalize.** M1 handles pasted text only: clean up whitespace, split the chain on Outlook's reply headers (the From / Sent / To / Subject blocks) wherever the paste contains them, and fold older messages for display. M2 adds what needs a real message: MIME parsing, preferring the text part, stripping HTML, and dropping signatures and legal footers. The full original is kept for display.
2. **Rules (M1).**
   - Title: the subject field with FW and RE prefixes removed. Empty if no subject was given.
   - Requested-by: the sender in the earliest From block found in the paste. Empty if the paste has no header blocks.
   - Ticket refs: INC, CHG, RITM, REQ, PRB, TASK, SCTASK and CTASK followed by digits, matched on word boundaries with the longest prefix first, so SCTASK0012345 is never read as TASK0012345. On the board each ref is a link built from one ServiceNow address setting. The implementation owner must prove the link format for each prefix on the instance.
   - Due date: filled only when the text holds exactly one absolute date next to a cue such as "by", "due", "deadline" or "no later than". A date that states its year is taken as written. A month and day with no year needs an anchor: the Sent date of the message that holds the quote, read from that message's header block in the paste (M1) or from the message itself (M2). With an anchor, the date resolves to its first occurrence on or after the anchor, and the field is filled only if that falls within 120 days of the anchor. With no anchor, or outside that window, the field stays empty. It also stays empty for more than one candidate, a relative date ("by Friday"), or an ambiguous numeric format ("11/12"). Every unfilled candidate is listed with its quote for the person to pick.
3. **Model pass (M3, only if an approved endpoint exists).** Send the normalized chain with a JSON schema: title, short_description, requested_by, due_date, action_required, affected_systems. For every field the model must also return the supporting quote and the message number it came from.
4. **Validate.** Reject any field whose supporting quote is not a verbatim substring of the email. A model-returned due date is kept only if it equals what the due-date rule above derives from its supporting quote. A field is either empty or backed by a quote, never guessed.
5. **Confirm.** A person reviews the draft next to the email (Premise 3). Owners are never filled unless the email names a person in the roster, because a wrong owner sends the ask to the wrong person.

Cache results by content hash so the same text is not processed twice. Build a test set from real emails (the three from the Assignment, grown to ten or more). Keep it out of this repo, in a company-approved location only the author can read.

### Stored email: access and retention

- An original email is visible to Lead and Triage. Managers see the published fields and the updates. Sharing is decided per email: each email has its own switch, off by default, set when it is confirmed or attached. Turning it on shows that one email to the firedrill's current owners. An email attached later starts unshared, whatever the others are set to. Adding an owner shows them the emails already shared, and the add-owner step says so.
- Rejecting an intake item ("not a firedrill") deletes its raw content and keeps only the hash.
- Raw content of a closed firedrill is deleted 90 days after it closes. The 90 days is a setting and a placeholder until the org's retention policy is known (Open Question 13).
- A draft that is neither published nor rejected within 30 days is deleted with its raw content and extraction result. Mail in the "Not accepted" folder is deleted after 30 days. A raw email reply is deleted 30 days after it was posted as an update. The purge applies the same periods to the copies in the shared mailbox: "Not accepted" and "Automatic" mail after 30 days, a processed reply 30 days after it was posted, and a processed intake email when its intake item's raw content is deleted.
- A daily purge job, shipped in M1, applies these rules. The extraction result is always deleted together with the raw content it was drawn from. A purge run that fails shows in the board banner and runs again the next day.

### Updates and asks

- **Request update, M1:** a `mailto:` link prefilled with the owner, a subject and four links to the update screen, one per status (on track, at risk, blocked, done). The body says to pick a link and that in M1 email replies are not tracked. The boss hits send in Outlook, so no mail permission is needed. The app records the ask when the button is clicked, which means an ask he never sends still shows as asked. Accepted for M1. A mailto body is plain text, so the four links appear as four short URLs. The implementation owner must prove in the boss's Outlook that the prefilled body survives and that the chosen status survives sign-in. If either fails, the ask falls back to a single link.
- **A manager replies by email anyway (M1).** Lead or Triage can post an update for that owner. It is stored with the poster as author and the owner in `on_behalf_of`, shows as "posted for" on the board, and answers the ask.
- **Request update, M2:** the app sends the email itself through the internal SMTP relay or Graph. It is sent from the app's shared mailbox under the display name "Situation Room". Its first line names the primary Lead it was sent for and says whether a person clicked or the board sent it on its own. The boss's own address is never the sender, which would need a wider grant than the one mailbox. It carries the same four status links, and it says that a reply will be posted to the board where the other managers can read it. Its Reply-To is the app's mailbox, and it carries a reference the app can match to the assignment. Whether that reference rides in the subject or in the address is Open Question 16. If no candidate works on this mail system, reply-to-update does not ship. Reply-to-update also has a switch in settings, so it can be turned off after launch without a deploy. Whenever it is off, for either reason, an app-sent ask sets its Reply-To to the primary Lead, says that replies are not tracked and to use the links, and a reply is handled as in M1: a Lead or Triage posts it for the owner. A reply to an ask that was sent before the switch was turned off still reaches the app's mailbox and is handled as described under Replies.
- **Reply-to-update, M2:** a manager answers an app-sent ask by replying. The poller matches the reference and the sender to the assignment, drops the quoted thread and the signature, and posts the remaining text as an update labeled "via email". If the first non-empty line is exactly one of the four status words, in any letter case and ignoring one trailing period, exclamation mark, comma or colon, that becomes the status and the rest is the text. A first line that ends in a question mark never matches. Otherwise the status stays as it was. A reply that is only a status word posts that status with no text. The manager gets a receipt that quotes exactly what was posted and links to the update screen, where a new update supersedes it. If that receipt fails or expires, the update is flagged "receipt not delivered" on the board and on the Health page, so a Lead or Triage can check it with the owner. The receipt also states the status as it now stands and repeats the four status links, since most replies open with a greeting and will not change the status. Nothing is posted, and the sender gets a notice instead, when the reply has no usable text, when the assignment has ended or is done, or when the firedrill is closed. A reply cannot be trusted to mean "reopen", so the notice for a done assignment links to the form, which is the only place a done assignment can be made active again. A reply to an older ask on the same assignment counts as an update to that assignment. Stripping quoted text from Outlook replies is fragile, so the raw reply is kept for 30 days, replies are part of the extraction test set, and the implementation owner must prove the stripping on real replies before this ships. Graph's `uniqueBody` is one candidate, not verified here.
- **Automatic asks, M2:** each 7:00 run on a working day creates one ask, for the primary Lead, for every assignment that meets all of these: it is active and stale that morning, it is not waiting, its firedrill's `auto_ask` is on, the global switch is on, it has no open ask, and no automatic ask exists yet for its current stale period. The row then shows "Auto-asked". An open ask is one that is unanswered, not cancelled, and whose latest send is pending or sent (an M1 mailto ask has no send and is open until answered or cancelled). An ask whose latest send failed or expired is unsent: its row shows "Ask failed", it is not open, and it is left out of the 70% figure. A stale period is named by the date its stale clock started counting (the newest update, the assignment's creation, the end of a wait or the firedrill's reopening), which `update_request.clock_start` records, and it ends at the next update or wait. The board sends at most one automatic ask per stale period. The rule is therefore "stale now and not yet asked in this period", not "turned stale today": an assignment missed by a failed run is asked on the next run. Turning the global switch on, or a firedrill's `auto_ask` back on, never causes a burst: an assignment that was already stale at that moment is not asked automatically for its current stale period. It stays on the board as stale, where a Lead can ask by hand. The same holds when N is lowered: an assignment made stale by the change is not asked automatically for its current stale period. Request update is always available on an active assignment and always creates a new ask, even while another ask is open, and one update answers them all. An assignment that is never answered is therefore asked automatically once, then stays "Auto-asked, no reply" on the board and in the digest until a person asks again, and Request update still works on that row. No automatic ask is sent when an open ask already exists, while the assignment is waiting, for a firedrill whose `auto_ask` is off, or while the global switch is off. The global switch starts off and is turned on only after the boss agrees (Open Question 15). Each stale assignment gets its own email, because a reply has to match one assignment, so a manager with several stale assignments gets several emails on one morning. The app does not know public holidays: automatic asks follow Monday to Friday, and the global switch is the way to pause them.
- **Monday digest, M2:** every Monday at 7:00 in the app's time zone the primary Lead gets one email listing, in the board's own order, open needs, firedrills overdue or due this week, stale assignments, and firedrills published in the last seven days, each linking to the board. If all four lists are empty it sends a short all-clear. If the 7:00 run fails, the five-minute job creates the digest at its next run after 7:30 that Monday (see Sending and failures). There is one digest per Monday, and one not sent by the end of its Monday is skipped. Triage can switch the digest off in settings.
- **Sending and failures, M2:** every email the app sends has a `mail_send` row written before the send, in state pending. The five-minute job sends pending mail and retries a failed send up to three times over an hour, then marks it failed. Before each attempt it checks that the email still applies: an ask that has been answered or cancelled is not sent, and an automatic ask is also not sent when its assignment is no longer stale or is now waiting. In each case the send is marked cancelled, and an automatic ask that no longer applies is cancelled with it. Every kind of mail has an `expires_at`: 24 hours after creation for an ask, a receipt, a notice and an alert, and the end of its Monday for a digest. Pending mail past its expiry is marked expired and shown on the Health page, whose retry action is offered only for mail that still applies, and never for an expired digest. The 7:00 scheduler only creates the rows. If no successful `scheduler` run is recorded for a working day by 7:30, the five-minute job does that work at its next run and records it as that day's `scheduler` run, so it happens once. An automatic ask is unique per assignment and `clock_start` in the database, and a digest is unique per Monday date, so two runs cannot create two. A crash between sending and recording can still send one duplicate, which is accepted. An ask whose latest send failed or expired is unsent: it is not open and does not count as asked. At the next 7:00 run an unsent automatic ask gets a new `mail_send` when its assignment is still active, stale and not waiting in the same stale period, both switches are on and it has no open ask. Nothing creates a second automatic ask for that period. An unsent ask that a person clicked is not retried: its row shows "Ask failed" and Request update works on it again. Failed sends show in the same banner as intake failures.
- **Asked, no reply:** an open ask, as defined under Automatic asks. An update answers every ask on its assignment that is unanswered and not cancelled, whatever the state of its send: each one's `answered_by_update` is set to that update in the same transaction. That stored link is the only definition of answered. An ask is cancelled, not answered, when its assignment ends, when its firedrill closes, or when a Lead or Triage cancels it from the row, for example an M1 ask whose email was never sent. Cancelled and unsent asks are left out of the 70% figure.

### Login and roles

- **Default: OIDC in the app** against the org's identity provider (Entra ID, or ADFS or Keycloak in front of on-prem AD). Group claims map to roles and the app validates tokens itself.
- **M1 alternative if client registration is slow:** the OpenShift oauth-proxy sidecar, which reuses cluster login and passes `X-Forwarded-User` and `X-Forwarded-Email`. This path exists only if the boss and the managers can sign in to the cluster at all (Open Question 11). Admission is limited with the proxy's `--openshift-sar` rule: only users bound to a role in the app's project get through. I found no documentation of a groups header, so roles come from the `person` table. Two 2026 advisories (CVE-2026-49331, CVE-2026-49332) describe identity-header spoofing through this proxy. If you use it: run a fixed version, avoid `skip-auth-regex`, and accept the header only from the sidecar.
- **Where roles come from:** the app, the poller and the seeding of the first Triage person all authorize on one field, `person.role`. When OIDC supplies group claims, each sign-in writes the mapped role into that field, so the directory stays the source and the claim wins over a value set by hand. A sign-in whose claims map to no role is refused. Without group claims, Triage sets the role by hand. Either way the user must have an active `person` row.
- **Roles:**
  - Lead (the boss and delegates): read everything including original emails, start a firedrill (paste, upload or forward), request updates and cancel an ask, confirm drafts, retry or reject a failed intake item, turn an email's sharing with owners on or off, add, remove or reassign owners, close and reopen, post an update for an owner, mark a need handled, set or clear waiting, switch automatic asks off or back on for a firedrill.
  - Manager: read all firedrills, open and closed (published fields and updates), and the original emails shared with them as an owner. On their own assignments: post updates, set or clear waiting, and raise or withdraw a need.
  - Triage (the author): everything Lead can do, plus edit any field, manage the roster and change settings, including the switches for automatic asks, reply-to-update and the Monday digest, and use the Health page with its retry and dismiss actions.

### API

- `GET /api/firedrills`, `GET /api/firedrills/{id}`
- `POST /api/firedrills/{id}/updates`
- `POST /api/firedrills/{id}/update-requests`

The author decided at the final gate on 2026-11-11 that the JSON form is not built in Milestone 1. It is added with its first caller. The routes above show the shape it takes on that day and are not M1 work. In M1 the pages are the only interface: server-rendered HTML handlers behind the login, which call one domain module. M1 ships no `/api/` routes, no OpenAPI document, no idempotency keys for JSON callers and no JSON authorization tests. The JSON form has no caller today, and no outside tool could call it until service tokens exist. When a named caller appears, the JSON form is added as a thin second representation over the same domain module, under the contract in "Developer experience amendments", with service tokens if the caller is not a signed-in person. The list is illustrative, not complete. Updates and asks are addressed to one assignment of the firedrill, named in the request, and marking a need handled, setting a wait and switching automatic asks each have their own handler.

An intake endpoint for other systems (`POST /api/intake` with service tokens) is not in a committed milestone. The original idea had Power Automate calling it, and Premise 4 rules that out, so it has no caller today. Add it when a named system needs to post, for example ServiceNow. It would create drafts only (Premise 3).

### State and operations

- Postgres from whatever the org offers: a database service, or an operator on the cluster. Prove one restore from backup before launch.
- The app holds no state. Run two replicas.
- The mailbox poller runs as a single CronJob on a short interval, five minutes by default, so two pods never process the same message. This is the "five-minute job": each run pulls the mailbox, sends pending mail, and makes up a missed 7:00 run. Processing is idempotent on message id regardless, for intake and for replies.
- A scheduler CronJob (M2) runs at 7:00 on working days, with the app's time zone set explicitly on the job. It creates the rows for automatic asks and, on Mondays, the digest, and the five-minute job sends them. Each send is recorded before it goes out (see Sending and failures), so a job that runs twice does not send twice and a failed send is retried, not lost. The 6:00 and 7:00 times rely on the cluster's CronJob time-zone field, which is asked about with the M0 requests. Without that field, each job runs hourly and the app's configured zone decides when the 6:00 and 7:00 work is due.
- A daily purge CronJob (M1) runs at 6:00, applies the retention rules and, on Fridays, stores the week's figures in `weekly_figure`.
- A Health page for Triage shows when each job last ran and how it ended (`job_run`), failed and expired sends with a retry action, not-accepted mail with the reason (`inbound_message`), refused sign-ins with the value presented, and the Friday figures for the last eight weeks. In M1 it holds the purge, refused sign-ins and the figures. M2 adds the five-minute job, the scheduler, sends and not-accepted mail. An alert reaches the maintainer when the purge, the five-minute job, the scheduler or a send goes from working to failing, and once more when it recovers. A job that keeps failing does not alert again on every run. Sending is judged as a whole: it is failing when the latest send attempt failed, and recovered at the next success. Alerts go through the cluster's alerting where it is offered (Open Question 19), and the alerts depend on it: ask for it with the M0 requests. Without it, an alert about the purge or the scheduler goes by email to the maintainer's alert address once the app can send, delivered by the five-minute job. An alert about sending, or about the five-minute job itself, cannot be delivered by the app, because it would travel the path that failed: it shows on the Health page and the banner, and the runbook tells Triage to check that page each working day. Four short runbooks cover an expired mailbox grant, a relay that rejects mail, a restore from backup, and a person who cannot sign in. Each part ships with the milestone it covers: M1 for sign-in, database and purge, M2 for mail.
- Secrets live in OpenShift Secrets, never in the repo.

### Milestones

| | What ships | Effort | Waits on |
|---|---|---|---|
| M0 | The Assignment first, then filing the requests for M1 and M2 | This week, about a day of your time | Nothing |
| M1 | Board with "Needs you", overdue and waiting. Roster, paste intake with rules, confirm, manager update with status links and "Seen by", ticket links, mailto ask, login, roles, audit log, the Health page with alerts and runbooks for the M1 parts, the Closed filter with Reopen, deployed | human: ~3.5 weeks / CC: ~2.5 days | Namespace, database, login (OIDC client or cluster login), role source (AD groups or the roster), image build and registry, code location, and a security review sign-off if the org requires one (Open Question 18) |
| M2 | .eml upload, mailbox pull, app-sent asks, reply-to-update, automatic asks, Monday digest, and the Health page, alerts and runbooks for mail | human: ~4 weeks / CC: ~2.5 days, not counting the seven tenant proofs listed under Open Question 16 | App registration, mailbox scope with read and write, permission to send mail as the shared mailbox (SMTP relay or Graph), egress to Graph |
| M3 | Model-drafted fields with quote validation | human: ~1 week / CC: ~half a day | Approved model endpoint |
| M4 | History: a per-firedrill timeline of updates, asks and owner changes | Later | M2 |

All effort figures are estimates. The DX review of 2026-11-11 adds a local run path, a configuration reference, an error contract and documentation to M1 (see "Developer experience amendments"). That work is not in the M1 figure above: allow about human ~1 week / CC ~half a day more. M0, M1 and M2 were re-estimated on 2026-11-11 to include the operations slice and the amendments from the /autoplan review. The JSON form left M1 at the final gate on the same day. The M1 figure was not lowered for it, so the figure carries that small margin.

If M1's prerequisites are not granted within three weeks of filing, ship Approach A for the boss and keep the requests open. The security review, where one is required, is one of those prerequisites and counts toward the three weeks.

M1 work does not start until the Assignment is done. If the boss does not confirm that he wants one place for firedrill status that his managers update, work stops and the approach is revisited before anything is built. M1 work also waits for three written answers, because a no to any of them changes the design or stops it: where the code may live (Open Question 8), whether copies of email may be stored (Open Question 13) and whether a security review is required (Open Question 18). A required review's sign-off is a prerequisite for going live, not for starting. If copies of email may not be stored, the fallback design keeps only the confirmed fields and their supporting quotes, and that redesign is settled before M1 starts.

M2 work starts as soon as its approvals land. It does not wait for the four-week check on M1 (see the stop rule under Success Criteria).

## Open Questions

1. What did the boss ask for, in his words?
2. May managers see each other's firedrills? The design assumes yes. Ask the managers as well as the boss: a status every peer can read may be written more defensively. The Assignment asks it. If the answer is no, a manager's board shows only the firedrills they own, the "Needs you" list is shown to Leads and Triage only, and the M2 ask says a reply is visible to the boss and to that firedrill's other owners. Co-owners of one firedrill still see its whole row, including each other's lines.
3. Entra ID or on-prem AD? This decides the OIDC provider and how group claims arrive.
4. Can pods reach graph.microsoft.com, directly or through an egress proxy?
5. Is there an approved model endpoint reachable from the cluster, and may email content be sent to it?
6. Which database offering, CI system and image registry does the org use?
7. Is there an internal SMTP relay the app may send through?
8. Where should this code live? The repo's remote is a personal GitHub account. Work code, and above all real emails, may belong in company source control. Check policy before pushing anything work-specific.
9. Does the boss want a firedrill to close on its own once every owner reports done? The design keeps it open until Lead or Triage closes it.
10. Is three working days the right staleness threshold? His call.
11. Can the boss and the managers sign in to the OpenShift cluster? If not, the oauth-proxy path for M1 does not exist and login waits on the OIDC client.
12. Which Outlook does the boss use? Classic Outlook for Windows saves messages as .msg, while new Outlook, Outlook on the web and Outlook for Mac give .eml (general product knowledge, not checked here). If he can only produce .msg, file upload is dropped and paste plus mailbox pull cover intake.
13. What do the org's data classification and retention rules say about storing copies of internal email? This sets the retention period and may limit which emails can be stored at all.
14. What does sign-in present for the boss and for one manager: the value of `X-Forwarded-User` under cluster login, or the username and email claims under OIDC? Does either equal their mailbox address? `login_id` stores whatever it turns out to be.
15. Does the boss agree to three things: asks going out for him without a click, managers seeing how long their needs have waited ("Needs you"), and managers seeing when he has viewed an update ("Seen by")? Automatic asks stay switched off until he agrees. If he says no to the first outright, automatic asks are not built in M2 and are recorded in TODOS.md. If he says not yet, they are built and stay off. Either way the ask a person clicks and the Monday digest are unaffected. If he declines the second, the "Needs you" list is shown to Leads and Triage only, and a manager still sees on their own row and update screen whether a need they raised was handled, by whom and when, and can still withdraw it. If he declines the third, "Seen by" is not built. His answers are known before M1 starts, because the Assignment is the gate.
16. Can a reply to an app-sent ask be matched back to its assignment on this mail system, by a tag in the subject or by plus addressing? Reply-to-update depends on it, and the implementation owner must prove it on the tenant. Matching the reply's In-Reply-To header, or Graph's conversation id, against the sent ask is a third candidate, not verified here. Seven tenant proofs are owed before the M2 build, each with what happens if it fails. Reply matching: reply-to-update does not ship. Reply stripping: reply-to-update does not ship. Sending as the shared mailbox: app-sent asks, automatic asks and the digest are held, and mailbox intake can still ship. Recognizing automatic mail: reply-to-update does not ship. Marking the app's own mail as automatic (Graph may accept only custom headers on send, not verified here): send through the relay instead, and if that fails too, the notice cap and the check for automatic mail on the way in are the loop protection. Reading the authenticated sender and not the display From: mailbox intake and reply-to-update do not ship, because both trust the sender. A message id that survives a folder move: if none does, handled mail is not moved and is tracked only in `inbound_message`.
17. Will managers answer by replying to an email? Reply-to-update rests on that habit, and nobody has checked it. The Assignment asks two or three managers. If they would rather click a link, ask every manager the same question before the reply-to-update work in M2 starts, and record the answers under Demand Evidence. If most prefer the link, the ORDER decision goes back to the author before that work starts.
18. Does the org require a security or architecture review before a new internal app may store copies of email? If it does, its sign-off is a prerequisite for M1 going live and may have the longest lead time of all. Ask during M0, before any M1 code.
19. Does the cluster offer alerting for user workloads? The M1 alerts depend on it. Without it, M1 failures show only on the Health page and the banner until the app can send mail in M2.

## Success Criteria

Proposed targets, measured from the app's own tables four weeks after M1 goes live.

- The boss opens the board on at least 3 working days per week (`usage_event`). This is measured in M1. Once the Monday digest ships in M2, the email may replace some visits, so the author reviews the target with the boss then.
- At least 80% of active assignments that are not waiting are fresh, checked each Friday. Active is defined under Data model: the firedrill is open and the assignment is neither done nor ended. Waiting assignments are reported as their own count and left out of this figure and of the stop rule, so parking work cannot meet the target.
- At least 70% of asks are answered within one working day, on the form or, from M2, by an email reply. Automatic asks count as asks. An update that a Lead or Triage posts for an owner does not count as an answer here, and cancelled asks are left out. "Within one working day" means before the end of the next working day after the day the ask was sent, so an ask sent on a Friday is on time through Monday.
- From M2, three more figures are reported each Friday, with no target until a month of data exists: the share of updates that arrive by email reply, the share of automatic asks answered within one working day, and the median age of open needs. The share of email replies that the manager then corrected is tracked as the misread rate, where a correction is a form update by the same owner within one working day of their email-reply update. Every Friday figure is computed by the daily job at its 6:00 run on Fridays, stored in `weekly_figure` and shown on the Health page. The freshness figure is a snapshot at that run. The ask figures cover asks whose one-working-day window closed in the seven days before the run, on assignments that were active when asked, leaving out cancelled and unsent asks. If the Friday run fails, the next successful run computes that week's figures and marks them late.
- In week four the boss sends no status-request emails outside the app (his own report), set against the baseline count taken at the Assignment. No firedrill passes its due date without the board having shown it as due.
- Extraction: on the test set, zero wrong values for title, due date and ticket refs. Empty is fine, wrong is not. Median human time from paste to publish is under two minutes.
- Stop rule: if fewer than half of active assignments that are not waiting are fresh after four weeks, stop adding features and go talk to the managers. M2 is not held for this check, and reply-to-update is finished even if the rule fires, because it is the remedy for what the rule measures. The rule pauses everything after M2.

## Distribution Plan

A container image built by the org's CI on merge to main, pushed to the internal registry, and deployed from manifests kept in this repo: Deployment, Service, Route with TLS, CronJobs for the purge (M1), the poller and the scheduler (M2), alert rules for the cluster's alerting where it is offered, and a pre-deploy Job for database migrations, which also creates the first Triage person. The manifests only reference Secrets by name. The Secrets themselves are created in the cluster out of band, or through the org's secret mechanism if it has one, and no credential is ever committed. The CI system and registry are Open Question 6. Until that is answered, an OpenShift BuildConfig building this repo's Dockerfile is the stopgap.

## Dependencies

- For M1: an OpenShift project with quota, a Postgres database, login (an OIDC client registration, or cluster login through oauth-proxy), a role source (AD groups for Lead, Manager and Triage, or the roster table), an image build and registry, a decision on where the code lives, and a security review sign-off if the org requires one (Open Question 18). Going live also needs the org's answer on storing copies of email (Open Question 13), which counts toward the three weeks: no real email is pasted in before it. M1 goes live on the day the first real firedrill is published, and the four-week figures count from that day. If the answer forbids keeping copies of email, the fallback design named under Milestones applies.
- For M2: a shared mailbox, an Entra app registration, an Exchange admin to scope it to that mailbox with RBAC for Applications (and to remove any tenant-wide mail grant, which would bypass the scope), read and write access on that mailbox, permission for the app to send mail as that shared mailbox (through the SMTP relay or Graph), and egress to Graph.
- For M3: approval to use a model endpoint with email content.

File the M1 and M2 requests during M0, after the Assignment: a shared mailbox and the right to send mail are not requested for a tool the boss has not yet confirmed he wants. File them the same week, because the M2 ones have the longest lead time and the three-week fallback clock starts at filing. Before filing, spend two hours checking what the tenant already gives the boss (ServiceNow dashboards, Planner, Lists) and write down what each cannot do. At the visit, also ask him who the second Triage person will be, since intake stops when the only one is on leave, and whether this is sanctioned work with a named owner in the org.

## The Assignment

Before any code: sit with your boss for about an hour with three real firedrill emails from his inbox. Start with open questions before you show the wireframe: what he wants, how he tracks firedrills today, and whether he would rather have a shared list this week or an app in six to eight weeks. Write down three numbers as the baseline: how many firedrills are open today, how many status requests he sent in the last two weeks (his Sent Items will show), and how many firedrills ran late without his knowing. Before the visit, write down which answers would stop the app, so that the gate can fail. Fill in the draft screen by hand for each one, together. Write down four things: his exact words for what he wants to see, which fields he cared about and which he ignored, which manager or managers own each firedrill (does each have exactly one?), and one firedrill he remembers being dropped or going stale. The same day, show the update screen to two or three managers and write down the first thing each one says. Once Open Question 13 is answered, the three emails become the first extraction test cases. Until then they stay in his mailbox and no copy is taken. What you hear replaces the guesses in Demand Evidence.

The CEO review added two short sets of questions to that visit. Ask your boss three yes-or-no questions: may the board send asks for him without a click, may managers see how long their needs have been waiting, and may they see when he has viewed their update. Ask each manager one more: would you rather answer by replying to an email or by clicking a link? Ask him and them whether managers may see each other's firedrills (Open Question 2). While you are there, ask your boss what he has tried before (a list, a spreadsheet, a ServiceNow view) and note which Outlook he uses. If he tried a shared list and dropped it, write down why under Status Quo: Approach A then stops being the fallback unless that reason no longer applies, and another fallback is chosen before M1 starts. The visit checks the premises the plan leans on most: that he asked for this, that nothing shared has been tried, and how managers will answer. Three other unverified claims are checked elsewhere: which file type his Outlook saves (Open Question 12, noted at this visit), whether a route on the cluster can be reached from Microsoft's cloud (asked with the M0 requests, and the plan does not depend on the answer, because intake is pull either way), and what the two oauth-proxy advisories say (read them before choosing that login path). Redraw the wireframe before this visit (see "Design amendments") and show the new frames: the old sketch contradicts the plan in several places.

This visit is a gate, not a courtesy. No milestone 1 work starts before it. If he does not confirm that he wants one place for firedrill status that his managers update, stop and revisit the approach before building anything.

## What I noticed about how you think

- You arrived with the wall already mapped: "I don't have any of the premium flows, so if we explore that then only use the built in and standard flows." Most people find that limit three weeks into the build.
- You had ruled out the obvious fix before proposing anything: "They also come from different sources, so it's not like we can set up an email filter for a certain DL."
- You described the result from your boss's chair, not as a feature list: "so my boss can go one place and get his updates."
- I recommended starting with a Microsoft List and you picked the custom app without saying why. Write the reason down. Someone at work will ask "why not a SharePoint list?" and the answer should be ready before they do.
