<!-- autoplan-accepted:eng -->
- **Engineering amendments accepted by the /autoplan engineering pass on 2026-11-11.** They say how the rules above are built so that they hold under two replicas, three jobs and a restore. Where one refines an earlier amendment, it says so. Two were marked provisional, and the author confirmed both at the final gate on 2026-11-11, along with every provisional amendment in the earlier lists.
  - One owner for assignment changes: a single domain module owns every change to an assignment (post an update, create an ask, set or clear a wait, end, mark done). Pages, jobs and the poller call it and never write those tables directly. Each of those operations locks the assignment row first and recomputes staleness under the lock, so an ask can never commit just after the update that answered the others. "May this assignment be asked now" is one function, read by the button, the scheduler and the digest. Check: a test on real Postgres pauses the scheduler between its read and its write, lets an update land, and ends with no open ask.
  - Guards on every transition: publish, attach, Handled, Close, Reopen, cancel an ask and Send are conditional updates that check the state they expect and the number of rows changed, and each form carries a one-time token under a unique constraint. Partial unique indexes enforce one unended assignment per firedrill and owner, one update per reply message id, one automatic ask per assignment and `clock_start`, and one digest per Monday. Removing the last unended owner is refused. Check: two simultaneous publishes of one draft yield one firedrill, and a repeated Send yields one update.
  - Burst suppression is stored: when the global switch, a firedrill's `auto_ask`, N or the non-working dates change, the app writes a cancelled automatic ask for every assignment that is stale at that moment. The unique key then suppresses that stale period, and the retry of unsent asks skips cancelled ones. Check: switch on with three stale assignments, run 7:00 twice, zero mail.
  - Fields added: `update_request.confirmed_sent_at` (the "Sent it?" answer), `update_request.batch_id` (one "Ask all" click), an automatic-reply time on the ask, and a `banner_dismissal` table keyed on the source row. `mail_send` points at what it is about through separate nullable keys with a check that exactly one is set, and an ask's latest send is derived, not stored on the ask. Check: the first migration holds these and the checks.
  - "No update yet" is shown when an assignment has no update, not when its status is `new`, so a first reply that names no status does not read as silence. Check: a status-less first reply shows its text with status "No update yet" replaced by "status not given".
  - Jobs in phases: the five-minute job runs catch-up, then send, then pull, each with its own time limit, error handling and `job_run` row, so a hung mailbox pull cannot stop mail going out. The daily job computes the Friday figures first, then purges the database, then the mailbox copies, each with its own outcome. A late freshness figure is a snapshot at the late run and is marked so. Check: with the mailbox unreachable, pending mail is still sent and the figures are still stored.
  - A job that never runs: an alert rule fires when a CronJob's last success is older than twice its interval. Without cluster alerting, any page load by a Lead or Triage evaluates the same test and raises the banner. This completes the "overdue" state. Check: suspend the five-minute CronJob and the banner appears within two intervals.
  - Sending, refined: transient failures (connect, sign-in to the relay, throttling) are retried with backoff until the mail's expiry, honoring Retry-After. This replaces "three times over an hour". Permanent failures fail at once. A bounce is permanent for that ask: it is not sent again and the row asks for a new owner or address. Sending health is judged on transport errors, not on one recipient's rejection. Each run sends at most a set number of mails. The Health page's retry only sets a row back to pending, and only a job sends. Check: a two-hour relay outage at 7:00 delivers that morning's asks afterwards, and a bounced ask is not re-sent the next day.
  - Sessions and sign-in: the session and the sign-in handshake values live in a signed, encrypted cookie or in the database, never in process memory, and the app reads the person's active flag and role on every request. The registration is single-tenant with issuer and tenant checked. The first sign-in binds the provider's immutable id to the person and later sign-ins match on it, through a maintained OIDC library. Under OIDC every forwarded-user header is ignored. Under oauth-proxy the app listens on localhost only, the Service exposes the proxy port only, and a NetworkPolicy closes the rest. The address to return to after sign-in must be a relative path. Check: a sign-in that starts on one replica and returns on the other succeeds, and a request with a forged user header under OIDC is anonymous.
  - Roles from the directory use app roles (the `roles` claim), not the groups claim, because a person in many groups receives no group list. A claim never demotes the last active Triage person. Under OIDC each person's role carries the date it was last confirmed at sign-in, and mailbox intake accepts a sender only when that date is within 30 days. Confirmed by the author, who accepts that a Lead who has not signed in for 30 days has a forwarded email refused. Check: a person in more than 200 groups signs in with the right role, and a sender whose role was confirmed 31 days ago is listed as not accepted with that reason.
  - Request forgery: every request that changes something carries a token and passes an Origin check, cookies are SameSite=Lax, and a test walks the route table so a new route without the check fails the suite. Check: a POST without the token is refused on every route.
  - One visibility policy: a single module answers which firedrills a person may see and whether a person may do an action to an object, denying by default, and every handler uses it. The answers to Open Questions 2 and 15 are named configuration keys. The test of role, ownership and each variant is generated from the route table, so an unlisted route fails. Check: with the restricted setting, a manager's request for another manager's firedrill by id is refused on every route.
  - Text from email: it is escaped first and highlights are added by position afterwards. The app sends a Content-Security-Policy with no inline script. Ticket links are built only from tokens the prefix rule matched, and the ServiceNow address must be https. Line breaks are stripped from anything placed in a mail subject, and every part of a mailto address is percent-encoded. Check: a script tag in every email-derived field, including the quote-highlight path, renders as text.
  - Mailto size: a test holds the encoded length of the ask under a set budget (1,800 characters until the M0 proof gives a real limit), and above it the ask falls back to one link. "Ask all stale owners" is held to the same budget. Check: a 200-character title still produces a link under the budget.
  - Mailbox intake, refined: messages are keyed on the mail system's immutable id, with the Message-ID header as a second signal, since a sender controls that header. Ids already handled are filtered out before the per-run cap is applied, a failed move is retried on its own and alerts after repeated failure, and the row and its effects commit in one transaction before the move. Only text parts are stored. Attachment names are recorded and attachments are not kept. One level of "forwarded as attachment" is unwrapped. Protected or encrypted mail is not accepted, with its own reason. A size cap applies. Check: one unmovable message does not stop newer mail from being handled.
  - Who sent a reply: the app accepts a reply only when the mail system marks it internal and authenticated, matches on the From address, and does not accept a reply sent on behalf of someone else. The reference in an ask is derived from the assignment with a keyed hash, is not guessable, and does not depend on the ask's row. At most ten email-reply updates are posted per assignment per day, and the rest are filed as not accepted. Check: a reply with a made-up reference posts nothing, and a reply to an ask whose row was lost in a restore still posts.
  - Reply stripping has a fixed fallback: the ask carries a "reply above this line" marker and the text is cut there first. If what remains still holds a From and Sent header block or is longer than a set length, nothing is posted and the manager gets the notice with the update link. Check: a reply typed below the marker posts nothing and draws the notice.
  - Restore, refined: the runbook's test is that the three replies are posted, no receipt is sent again during the replay, and any ask sent after the backup may be sent once more, which is accepted in writing. This replaces the earlier "no duplicate ask". Check: the rehearsal records how many asks were repeated.
  - Deploying: the migration Job is named for the image it runs, the pipeline applies it, waits for it to finish, and only then applies the Deployment. Readiness means the process is up, and the app serves its own 503 page when the database is down. One required configuration key holds the app's public address, checked at start, and every link in mail is built from it. The read-only part of the after-deploy check (health, database, a board for a seeded role) is a script the pipeline runs. Check: a deploy with a failing migration leaves the old version serving, and a wrong public address stops the app at start.
  - Requests added to M0: outbound access from the cluster to the identity provider's discovery, key and token addresses for M1, and to the token address as well as Graph for M2. An eighth tenant proof: the app's credential tries to read a different mailbox and is refused. The app registration uses a certificate or workload identity where offered. Its expiry date is configuration, the Health page warns 30 days ahead, and the alert rules include it. Check: the M0 request list names the provider addresses, and the eighth proof has a recorded result.
  - Due dates, closing earlier concerns R3-7 and R3-8: a date with no year is filled only when it falls in the calendar year of its anchor, and a date that would roll into the next year is listed as a candidate. A Sent line that is missing, unreadable or numeric and ambiguous is no anchor. Check: "the January 15 deadline was missed" in a November message fills nothing.
  - Retention, closing gaps: the audit log stores field names and ids for email-derived fields, not their values. The backup window is stated beside the retention periods, because backups keep deleted content for that long. Raw replies are readable by Lead and Triage only. Supporting quotes show on the confirm screen only. Opening an original email is written to the audit log. Check: after the purge, no table outside backups holds the email's text.
  - Small rules: a wait date must be in the future. A wait ends when its assignment is done. Publishing a draft the purge already deleted is refused with a message. A Lead's own view of an update on their own assignment does not mark it seen. The last board view time is written after the page is rendered. Refused sign-ins are stored once per value per day. Text typed before a session expired is kept in the browser and restored after sign-in, and no request is replayed. The development sign-in also refuses to start unless the app listens on localhost, and a check in CI fails if any manifest sets its flag. Check: one test per rule.
  - Tests run on Postgres with the app's clock: CI uses the compose file's Postgres, never SQLite, and every business timestamp comes from the app's settable clock, not from the database. Synthetic or redacted email fixtures live in the repo for CI. The private set of real emails is run by hand before each release and the result is recorded. Check: CI fails if a table default uses the database clock for a business timestamp.
  - Milestone 1 in two steps: M1a is a deployed walking skeleton (sign-in, roster, a firedrill created by hand, the board, the update form and the mailto ask), which shows the sign-in value, whether the route can be reached and whether the pipeline works in the first week. M1b is the rest of M1. Nothing is cut. Confirmed by the author. Check: M1a is deployed before M1b work on intake starts.
  - The JSON form, decided by the author at the final gate on 2026-11-11: it is not built in Milestone 1 and is added with its first caller. This revises two earlier amendments. The API line in the CEO amendments said M1 includes it until the question is decided: M1 no longer includes it. The "if it is built" line in the developer experience amendments stays as the contract for the day it is added. M1 has no `/api/` routes, no OpenAPI document, no idempotency keys for JSON callers and no JSON authorization tests. The error contract still covers pages, partial page updates and jobs, and a partial page update without a valid session still gets 401. The pages call the domain module above, so the JSON form stays a thin addition. Check: the M1 route table holds no JSON route, and no page handler holds a rule that the domain module does not.
- **Added by the engineering rerun on 2026-11-11, after the author's reply at the final gate.** A second independent reviewer read the plan with the JSON form out of Milestone 1. These refine the list above. One is provisional and says so.
  - One governing document comes first: folding the four amendment lists into the body is a precondition for M1a, not for M1 as a whole. Until then, where the body and an amendment disagree, the amendment governs. The body still says "three times over an hour" for retries, group claims for roles, the Message-ID header as the message key, a 120-day window for dates with no year, a stored latest send on the ask, one `about` field on `mail_send`, no knowledge of holidays, four runbooks and three job names. Check: a script fails the build while the governing document holds any of those superseded phrases.
  - Lock order: an operation that touches more than one assignment (Close, Reopen, "Ask all stale owners", burst suppression, the scheduler) locks the firedrill row first and then its assignments in id order, and the domain module refuses a write on a firedrill whose state changed under it. Check: Close and Send submitted together end with either a posted update on an open firedrill or a refused update on a closed one.
  - Estimate: the M1 figure does not include the engineering amendments (sessions and identity binding, forgery protection, the visibility policy with generated tests, the content policy, transition guards, phased jobs, deploy ordering). M1a and M1b are estimated separately before M1a starts, and the three-week fallback and any duration quoted to the boss use those figures. Check: the Milestones table holds a figure for M1a and one for M1b, each marked estimated.
  - One sign-in mode setting: a required setting names the mode as `oidc`, `proxy` or `dev`, checked at start. `dev` also needs a non-production environment marker. This replaces telling the modes apart by which other settings are present, since the app listens on localhost under both the proxy and the development sign-in. Check: one start-up test per mode, and `dev` refuses to start with the production marker.
  - The clock and the purge: the settable clock is refused at start outside `dev`, and the CI check that covers the development sign-in flag covers the clock setting too. The purge logs its counts before deleting and stops with an alert when a run would delete more than a set ceiling. Check: a start with the clock override in `oidc` mode fails, and a purge above the ceiling deletes nothing.
  - Purge boundaries are tested: days 29, 30 and 31 for each retention rule under the app clock, across a daylight-saving change and a year end, and an open firedrill's email is never selected. Check: that table test exists and passes.
  - The 30-day role confirmation, completed: a session has a maximum age (12 hours proposed) and is renewed by a silent sign-in, so a role is confirmed on any day the person uses the app. When a forward is refused for this reason, the sender gets a notice with the sign-in link, the Health page warns seven days before a Lead's confirmation runs out, and the refusal shows on the Lead's banner. Check: a forward from a Lead confirmed 31 days ago draws the notice, and a 13-hour-old session signs in again without a prompt.
  - A cap on automatic asks: if one run would create asks for more than a set share of active assignments, or more than a set number for one recipient, it creates none, alerts Triage and waits for a confirm on the Health page. This covers a long outage or an unlisted holiday week, which burst suppression does not. Provisional: the author confirms or reverses it at the final gate. Check: after a simulated five-day outage the first run sends nothing and raises the alert.
  - Mail with no text part: the app stores text derived from the HTML and never the HTML itself, and "original" on screen means that text. Check: an HTML-only fixture is stored as text and renders escaped.
  - A message that fails every time: `inbound_message` counts attempts. After a set number of failures it is marked failed, moved to a "Failed" folder and listed on the Health page. Check: a fixture that throws in parsing stops being retried and newer mail is handled.
  - The primary Lead is protected like the last Triage person: a claim never demotes them, an alert is raised instead, and the scheduler and digest fail with a named code when the setting does not resolve to an active Lead. Check: a claim that would demote the primary Lead changes nothing and raises the alert.
  - Identity, refined: the binding uses the provider's tenant id and object id, not `sub`, so recreating the app registration locks nobody out. The first bind is written to the audit log and Triage has an audited "clear binding" action. The email claim is compared at sign-in and a mismatch with the stored address shows on the Health page. People with no sign-in for 30 days are listed there, and the offboarding runbook has a step to deactivate them. Check: a sign-in with a changed `sub` and the same object id reaches the same person.
  - Senders, refined: the "internal and authenticated" test for replies applies unchanged to forwarded intake, and it is stated under Accepted senders. Check: a forward that the mail system does not mark internal and authenticated is listed as not accepted.
  - Public ids: each assignment and firedrill has a random public id. The reply reference and every address in an email are derived from it, never from the serial key, so mail already in inboxes cannot point at a different row after a restore. Check: the restore rehearsal shows an old link and an old reference resolving to the same assignment or to nothing.
  - Stored email at rest: the M0 database request asks whether the database and its backups are encrypted at rest and who can read them, and the answers are recorded with Open Questions 13 and 18. The confirm screen warns when pasted text looks like it holds a credential. The database layer never logs bound parameters, and the database owner is asked whether statement logging is on. Check: a forced database error with email text in a parameter leaves no email text in the pod log.
  - Browser rules: the content policy also forbids framing, and the app sends strict transport security and a referrer policy. The page library is served from the app with its evaluation and injected-style options off. A 401 on a partial update is turned into the sign-in redirect by one handler that keeps typed text. The only writes on a GET are seen marks, usage events and the last board view, and each is safe to repeat. Proved in M1a. Check: the board works with the content policy enforced and no console violation, and an expired session on a partial update lands on sign-in with the text restored.
  - `clock_start` is one pure function over an assignment's history (update, creation, wait end, early wait clear, reopen), covered by a table of histories and by property tests of the date function. Ties for "newest update" break on a database sequence, not on a timestamp. Check: the table test covers all five events.
  - Jobs, refined: at most one successful scheduler run is recorded per working date, alert state is defined per phase, and a suppression ask carries a cancel reason so it never shows as "Auto-asked" and the figures leave it out. Check: a catch-up and a slow 7:00 run on one date leave one success row.
  - Retention for housekeeping tables: the purge names a period for one-time form tokens, `inbound_message`, `mail_send`, `audit_log` and `usage_event`. Check: each of the five has a period in the configuration reference.
  - The mailto proof in M0 also covers several recipients with the separator Outlook accepts, and "Ask all stale owners" splits into more than one compose window above a recipient cap. Check: the proof records the separator and the cap.
  - More tests named: the previous image against the new schema and an upgrade with data both run in CI, reassign against Send, a reply posted while its owner is reassigned, two "Ask all" clicks, and recorded mail-system responses for paging, throttling with Retry-After, a token expiring mid-run and an id surviving a move. Check: each is in the test plan.
<!-- /autoplan-accepted:eng -->

### Final gate

APPROVED by the author on 2026-11-11. Gate revision 1: `rec` (uc1a, uc2a, uc3b, d1 to d15 kept). Gate revision 2: `rec` (d16a, p1a). The cap on automatic asks is confirmed, so no amendment in this plan is provisional any longer.
